# Unfilter digest 01 — baby and toddler sleep, 0–36 months

Prepared 2026-11-16 after reading the build README, the complete current findings, every assigned quote and each ledger’s **What these posts add** section. Only this digest is written.

| Assigned voice file | Quotes | Ledger |
|---|---:|---|
| `voice/reddit-ur02.jsonl` | 451 | [reddit-reading-ur02.md](../sources/reddit-reading-ur02.md) |
| `voice/reddit-ur11.jsonl` | 325 | [reddit-reading-ur11.md](../sources/reddit-reading-ur11.md) |
| `voice/reddit-ur13.jsonl` | 345 | [reddit-reading-ur13.md](../sources/reddit-reading-ur13.md) |
| `voice/reddit-ur19.jsonl` | 299 | [reddit-reading-ur19.md](../sources/reddit-reading-ur19.md) |

The share contains **1,420 quote records**. These posts were selected because earlier filtering excluded their subject matter. Counts describe quote records, never people, households, prevalence or success rates. Several excerpts may belong to one post or follow-up sequence. No network or git operation was used.

The revised findings already acknowledge medical uncertainty, coercion, rage, crisis, health constraints and diverse experiences of cosleeping. The additions here are missing mechanisms, sequences, boundaries and counter-examples. Medical explanations, treatment effects, safety claims, attachment claims and harm claims remain what the speaker reports or believes. Research does not decide which words belong in copy or convert the reports into advice.

The decision notes come first so the final writer can inspect the proposed changes before working through the evidence. Full quote-field text appears once per reproduced record, preserving spelling, punctuation, line breaks, profanity and sensitive details. Each evidence group ends with further supporting IDs. Money and Requests index the full verbatim evidence already reproduced in this file.

## Provenance and limits

- The narration behind [sbu11-0131](#sbu11-0131) is identified in its ledger as rewritten with ChatGPT. [sbu19-0050](#sbu19-0050) [sbu19-0051](#sbu19-0051) [sbu19-0052](#sbu19-0052) belong to one explicitly AI-assisted account. Preserve this provenance when using the failure-to-relief sequence.

- [sbu02-0404](#sbu02-0404) [sbu02-0405](#sbu02-0405) are a Luna app creator’s offer, labelled possible promotion. [sbu19-0127](#sbu19-0127) reports buying Herdmate sleepwear and repeats product claims; promotional intent is unconfirmed. These are not independently verified efficacy or willingness-to-pay evidence for a sleep plan.

- [sbu02-0036](#sbu02-0036) is labelled professional rather than parent testimony. Reviewer, advisor and relative-caregiver labels are also retained. Do not silently turn every speaker into a mother or a buyer.

- Cross-file wording overlaps: [sbu02-0117](#sbu02-0117) [sbu11-0143](#sbu11-0143) and [sbu02-0236](#sbu02-0236) [sbu11-0115](#sbu11-0115). In each pair, one quote field contains the other. Both IDs remain covered, but the overlap is not additional independent testimony.

## Pattern decisions

### 1. Parents sometimes want a parenting philosophy or an assessment of evidence as much as a sleep plan.

**Current findings:** §§1, 5.5, 6.3, 7, 10–11. **contradicts.**

**Current conclusion:** “She does not want a philosophy.”

**Also in the current findings:** “She trusts other parents in the same situation more than any expert”

**Change for the rewrite:** Replace the absolute with a range: some ask for executable steps, others are deciding what counts as responsive or Attachment Parenting, which risks they accept, and whose evidence deserves trust. The parent who explicitly rejects reassuring cosleeping anecdotes also contradicts the unqualified preference for peer testimony. These are attributed beliefs about research, not findings about harm. Do not make an ethical limit an obstacle to compliance.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-values).

### 2. Reported sleep hours, the baby's sleep, and the parent's ability to cope are separate outcomes.

**Current findings:** §§1–2, 9.1, 10, 17.7. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “It does not support promising that the parent's own sleep will come back when the baby's does;”

**Change for the rewrite:** Keep that qualification and extend it beyond insomnia. A mother reports six to seven uninterrupted hours but cannot cope with her shift; a GP repeats a six-hour target she already meets. Another denies sleep deprivation and general PPA/PPD yet fears her own intrusive thoughts after prolonged settling. Depression and impaired cognition can persist with good nights, while feeling better can precede independent naps. The SSRI benefit, difficult SSRI start and other treatment accounts are different reported experiences, not evidence of a uniform treatment effect. A parent's statement that she is not depressed or not sleep deprived must remain beside the crisis evidence.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-cope).

### 3. Some sleep requests occur amid suicidal thoughts, psychosis, dissociation, escape wishes or fear of continuing care.

**Current findings:** §§6.4, 9.1, 10. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “The restored evidence also includes hopelessness, panic, hallucinations, rage, reported harm toward a child and physical conditions that constrain care.”

**Change for the rewrite:** The new passages supply the actual care sequences and barriers: a reported psychotic admission followed by feeding and medication decisions; a self-harm attempt after a disputed handover; passive wishes not to wake; a mother withholding suicidal feelings from her perinatal team for fear of separation from her baby; and military-base stigma about therapy. Preserve explicit denials of self-harm, and distinguish a reported attempt, a passive death fantasy, intrusive thoughts, metaphoric exhaustion and deliberate withdrawal from an overwhelming situation. A single sleep explanation or a questionnaire document is not established as sufficient.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-crisis).

### 4. Parents describe feared harm, involuntary angry speech, rough handling and actual hitting as different events.

**Current findings:** §§4.5, 6.4, 9.1. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “The earlier account of guilt and lost identity omitted reported harshness and harm.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Retain the concrete actions and their context: holding a baby's bottom or arms down, blocking a toddler's exit, vigorous rocking, forcing a child back down, rough pacifier use, slapping a hand, rejecting a hug, locking a room, and hitting an eighteen-month-old three times after a collision. Some explicitly say they have never hurt their child and move away when afraid; others describe an act they regret. Several link their fear to their own childhood violence. Neither a rage label nor a general guilt summary captures these distinctions. A parent's theory that shouting finally produces sleep remains their account, not a technique.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-anger).

### 5. Fathers describe dread, overload, shame, rough handling and health fears in their own right.

**Current findings:** §§5.1–5.2, 9.2, 10. **contradicts.**

**Current conclusion:** “They frame the pain through the wife:”

**Also in the current findings:** “Interpretation: a plan a father can follow on his own speaks to them.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Rewrite the emphasis in §9.2. Fathers also describe being the primary night caregiver, anticipating a 10pm–3am shift with dread, losing confidence beside a partner who settles more easily, arriving home without remembering the drive, needing eight hours despite getting six, involuntary angry speech, rough handling, and a passive fantasy of being hit by a bus. One says the accumulation of responsibility and isolation, beyond lack of sleep alone, is the problem. Their own rest, bonding and health belong in the portrait alongside process-oriented success stories. These excerpts are not all independent households; preserve the sequences.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-fathers).

### 6. An agreed method or handover can be undermined by unequal work, control, intimidation or lack of support.

**Current findings:** §§5.1, 9.1, 9.6, 10, 16.7, 17.9. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “These are different from two willing caregivers needing the same script.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Add concrete control and aftermath. A partner says the mother needs a babysitter to enforce a sleep schedule and breaks a door while holding the baby; another invokes custody when the mother describes not coping. Some accounts concern ordinary exhausted disagreements, some a refusal to help, and some threats or intimidation. A mother leaving abuse describes losing the housing and routines that previously made her effective. Therapy is explicitly said not to replace an absent support system. Keep these distinct from a household that needs a clearer shared set of instructions. Do not infer abuse merely from an unconfirmed worry about marks on a baby.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-labor).

### 7. Rest, bodily autonomy, intimacy and feeling valued do not necessarily improve together.

**Current findings:** §§5.1–5.2, 9.1. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “The fight over who carries the load is sharper than the fight over method”

**Change for the rewrite:** Show what the lost evening means and where a task-sharing fix is insufficient. One mother says she is not a sex vending machine after her husband takes on more chores; others are touched out, miss the marital bed, feel rejected or want time without being a milk source. A father's successful overnight gift creates worry that telling his wife how easily the baby slept will diminish her effort. Easier settling can relieve work and still complicate a caregiver's sense of being needed.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-intimacy).

### 8. The preferred caregiver can change, and being rejected at sleep time hurts even when another adult helps.

**Current findings:** §§5.2–5.3, 9.2–9.3. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “The reverse is just as vivid.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Keep both directions and extend beyond a stable only-mum association. Rejection occurs after a C-section limits a mother's lifting, after illness or nursing changes, at a particular nap, or only when the other parent is present. Some grandparents settle a baby and others refuse another attempt after sustained screaming. A parent's access to breaks depends on whether the substitute actually works. A child who sleeps at daycare or with Dad is not evidence that the other caregiver lacks effort or knowledge.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-caregiver).

### 9. Parent and sibling health can make the usual handover, lifting or sleep arrangement physically unavailable.

**Current findings:** §§5.1, 9.1, 9.6, 10. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “a caregiver may be physically unable to sustain rocking, holding or a walking-based solution.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Make the constraint combinations explicit: reflux upright holding plus a painful C-section; a back injury and depleted partner PTO; a CPAP mask that moves during the proposed curl; sleepwalking while holding the baby; alternating entire nights around insomnia medication; neck injuries requiring an inclined adult mattress; pacemaker recovery; epilepsy triggered by sleep deprivation; and a sibling whose seizure trigger is interrupted sleep. A willing partner may still be unable to perform the proposed job. Advice to prioritise rest is not yet a practicable feeding or pumping plan.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-capacity).

### 10. Medical workups and treatment show partial benefit, failure, relapse, side-effect fears and unresolved causes.

**Current findings:** §§3, 4.1, 4.5, 6.2, 10, 17.9. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “medication and supplements that helped, failed or stopped helping”

**Change for the rewrite:** Retain the sequence, not only a list of things tried. Examples include repeated ferritin testing, low ferritin with normal iron and haemoglobin, split nights improving while three to ten wakes persist, no benefit after two months of supplementation, gasping noticed only during room sharing, nighttime oxygen after an apnoea diagnosis, repeated ear infections while waiting for tubes, and divergent ENT opinions with short-lived medication benefit. One parent associates a sleep collapse with Montelukast prescribed for an already sleep-disrupting cough. Another reports general anaesthesia before sleep disturbance. These reports do not establish causation or a reliable medical fix. Trial pain relief that does not help cannot establish the absence of pain.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-medical).

### 11. Feeding may provide needed intake, require sleep first, or become a medically forced night-weaning problem.

**Current findings:** §§3–4, 5.5, 6.1–6.2, 10, 17.9. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “Reported advice conflicts;”

**Change for the rewrite:** Distinguish feed-to-sleep as comfort from low daytime intake, bottle aversion, milk transfer, tube feeding and breastfeeding supply worries. A baby must be asleep to accept a bottle; the parent says normal weight gain hides the labour needed to achieve it. Reflux holding means the baby is already asleep before drowsy-but-awake instructions can start. One parent is told to suspend breastfeeding after treatment for an ectopic pregnancy, turning gradual weaning into an abrupt loss. Other parents keep night feeds on purpose or report no sleep improvement after weaning. Include medicine-giving while half asleep and consent questions without resolving them as clinical advice.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-feeding).

### 12. Sleep fear is sometimes anchored in bereavement, a breathing event, a seizure, a fall or failed equipment.

**Current findings:** §§2, 6.4, 9.1. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “Fears include death, harm, attachment and being unable to continue”

**Change for the rewrite:** Add the remembered events behind vigilance: loss and stillbirth, NICU alarms disappearing at discharge, a seizure minutes into a nap, gasping, a rail detaching, a fall after an arm and back become painful, a monitor display running out of power, and a bed rail used to reach a window ledge. After an event, the child may be asleep and medically reassured while the parent cannot rest. Prior loss can make closeness both especially desirable and frightening. A bereaved parent's sleeping beside her deceased son must stay in her own words, without converting that experience into a general sleep arrangement recommendation.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-fear).

### 13. Cosleeping can be a cherished choice, an exhausted compromise, a refused option or another failed fallback.

**Current findings:** §§2, 5.5, 9.5–9.6, 10. **confirms.**

**Current conclusion:** “Some value the closeness, some want to keep it while changing feeds or naps, some cannot sleep that way, and some reject both cosleeping and crying methods.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Keep the revised range and add the concrete boundaries: bedsharing can stop screaming without changing wake frequency, work at night while bedtime remains difficult, extend the morning only, or fail because the baby wants upright arms rather than lying nearby. Other babies dislike contact altogether. Some parents reject it because of their own movements, heavy-sleeping partners, medication, pets, premature birth, bed size or anxiety. Parents' claims that they follow safety rules, that their sleeping body protects the baby, or that the arrangement is safer remain attributed beliefs. A crib or floor bed is not an automatic substitute.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-cosleep).

### 14. Gentle labels, independent settling and consistency do not answer when to intervene or stop.

**Current findings:** §§4.2, 4.5, 6.1, 17.6, 17.9. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “A stop is not simply failure to persist.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Quote the actual response: choking or gagging, vomiting, breath holding, a lost voice, standing asleep, striking crib bars, a blowout, and scratching until bleeding. Some stop, some switch to patting or contact, some continue with their own limit, and some report improvement without reaching that limit. Pick-up/put-down can still produce prolonged sobbing and a twenty-eight-minute nap. One parent withholds a scheduled feed because the baby has not fallen asleep; another asks whether comforting is permitted during a wellness check. A method's internal rule and the child's care needs are different questions. This evidence does not supply a validated threshold.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-stopping).

### 15. Independent sleep onset and exact clock arithmetic can coexist with persistent waking, short naps or worsening nights.

**Current findings:** §§4.3–4.4, 6.1–6.2, 7, 17.2, 17.6. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “The same symptom has opposite fixes”

**Also in the current findings:** “Every reader who looked found the same order. First the wake windows or the nap caps.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Keep both directions. Parents cap naps, extend windows and preserve a nominal bedtime yet describe a tired child or worse night; another lets naps run and gets twelve hours without a waking. Some babies self-settle initially but cannot repeat it overnight. Others sleep after a parent visibly gives up, or after feeding and transfer. Questions cover nap transitions, correcting reverse cycling, first steps, what counts as awake time and what an intervention's response means. Routines that support work or a joint sibling nap may also be chosen for family feasibility. Do not turn observed sequences into an ordered protocol.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-schedule).

### 16. Illness, travel, movement and bed or sleepwear transitions interrupt progress in overlapping and unpredictable sequences.

**Current findings:** §§2, 9.3–9.4, 16.2, 17.2, 17.9. **confirms.**

**Current conclusion:** “Illness, feeding, travel, a bed move and treatment changes accompany the return of waking.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Add the sequences and different outcomes: rolling or standing, a changed sleep sack, weaning, daycare, several consecutive illnesses, travel without a crib, room sharing in a rental, a new sibling and a house move can overlap. A previously effective method may hold for a week, months, or fail on a second attempt. A twin can sleep through the same illness while the other cannot. Some returns improve with time and no changed method. The share supports planning for setbacks, not a prevalence estimate, a common duration or the assertion that parents universally change the clock first.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-setbacks).

### 17. Gear, temperature, body size and the layout of the home decide whether a proposed sleep setup is usable.

**Current findings:** §§4.6, 8, 9.4, 9.6, 10. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “These widen the fit requirement beyond age and willingness to follow the method.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Add precise fit failures and trade-offs: a bedside cot leaves a ten-to-fifteen-centimetre gap at an ottoman bed and is outside its return period; a broken crib cannot be lowered; the child is beyond equipment weight limits; a sleep sack traps arms or curls feet; an adult cannot reach a lower mattress comfortably; a helmet and heat rash rule out warming the surface; and a window air conditioner admits light during the only useful morning stretch. Cats, closed doors, outdoor air and a partner's disagreement interact with heat. New equipment can introduce a new climbing route or leave the original sleep problem intact.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-equipment).

### 18. Daycare rules, sibling needs and relatives' decisions constrain the plan and sometimes supply the workable solution.

**Current findings:** §§5.3–5.4, 9.3, 9.6, 10. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “That is another concrete limit on a family’s control of the plan.”

**Change for the rewrite:** Add institutional reasons as reported by speakers: staff need sleeping babies for breaks or tidying, a teacher says licensing prevents waking her own child, and a daycare permits a sleep sack but no swaddle. Those rules have not been independently checked. Some daycare naps work when home settling does not; others are absent or too short. Keeping siblings or twins together, protecting an older child's sleep, and preserving a synchronized nap can outweigh a textbook schedule. Relatives can provide real rescue and can also undo timing, pressure separation or criticize the family without taking over the consequences.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-daycare).

### 19. Monitors, trackers and AI can provide reassurance or create lost sleep, conflicting evidence and new care fears.

**Current findings:** §§4.3, 6.4, 7–8, 10. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “Monitors can misclassify waking or generate repeated alerts;”

**Change for the rewrite:** Join acquisition and aftermath: a literal panic purchase at four in the morning follows two hours of sleep, or a rolled baby's airway cannot be seen on video; later the sock reports shorter sleep, little deep sleep, lower scores or a back-only prompt that confuses the parent. Another monitor's repeated lost-reading alerts prevent rest despite longer baby stretches. A mother cannot tolerate sleeping beside her child because she loses camera-based sleep-hour inspection, while a tube-feeding parent doubts a monitor will reveal quiet vomiting. ChatGPT is consulted about aspiration after medicine given half asleep. Record these competing perceptions without adjudicating device readings or clinical questions.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-technology).

### 20. Paid help competes on listening, usable support, learning and durability, while purchases often follow a specific failed fallback.

**Current findings:** §§4.1–4.2, 4.6, 8, 10, 16.3, 17.3. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “A better document answers the generic-plan complaint. It does not answer the wish for someone to hold the line”

**Change for the rewrite:** Two gentle consultants reportedly conclude temperament is the problem; an expensive consultant is followed by ongoing wakes; one clinic helps parents understand sleep well enough to troubleshoot while another improves naps and worsens nights. Parents ask for a night nurse, a cook, a professional consultant or an agency, not only information. Housing, unpaid or depleted leave, insured treatment access and lost wages also shape what can be bought. The share supplies no exact course or consultant price and does not test the proposed Mamivio price band. The rent comparison is rent, not a sleep-plan anchor.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-paid).

### 21. Sleep loss consumes ordinary time, work, identity and the ability to parent, even without a new method question.

**Current findings:** §§1–2, 6.1–6.3, 9.1, 9.3–9.4. **confirms.**

**Current conclusion:** “the parent's own life is what they are trying to rescue”

**Change for the rewrite:** Keep the concrete scale of the need: eating, showering, a free arm, one evening, a bed beside a partner, being coherent at work, not driving dangerously, and having patience for another child. Some want solidarity or hope; some explicitly do not want another theory or lecture. This group's supporting records include short logs and recurring descriptions whose substance is already represented in fuller quotations. Do not read their frequency as prevalence.

[Verbatim evidence and further supporting IDs](#evidence-daily).

<a id="evidence-cope"></a>

> Our baby has been struggling a lot with reflux and hasn't been sleeping well and I've been dead exhausted. I normally wouldn't leave my baby with my husband, but I just can't function anymore so last night I put my headphones on to sleep and my husband took our baby to the living room for the night. I didn't notice anything out of the ordinary when I got up at 10 this morning besides that he once again forgot to change his diaper for like five hours and didn't give him his reflux medicine or vitamins.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0077"></a>
**[sbu19-0077]** — parent_unspecified; unknown; fear

<!-- quote:sbu19-0077 -->
> I don't want to think that he could've done something to our baby, but what is the other option here? I know mongolian spots are a thing, but do they just appear overnight like this? He gets frustrated with our baby after only a couple hours and passes him off to me. Did he get frustrated? Did he just pat his butt too hard? We got into a fight this morning over him forgetting the medicine so tension is already high and I'm worried I'm over reacting because of it. But I know I gave my baby to him last night at 1246am and he didn't have a single spot on him and at 10am he had them all over.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0093"></a>
**[sbu19-0093]** — partner; unknown; fear

<!-- quote:sbu19-0093 -->
> From thinking i have caused SBS (from rocking my little one too hard),to mistakenly picking her up by the neck while readjusting her on my lap (i was running low on sleep) I can’t seen to get a break from these intrusive thoughts thinking that I did something to my LO.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0285"></a>
**[sbu19-0285]** — expecting_mother; unknown; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0285 -->
> My toddler has been a difficult child since he was born. He's never slept through the night. He fights every single bedtime and cries every single time when he wakes up for up to an hour. Including today when I let him nap next to me for 30min, before waking him up, trying to get him to laugh and saying lets go eat soup. Somehow it worked and for 10 minutes he didn't cry. Then we sat down to eat, I was like here let mama give you all my olives I know you like them, he just starts screaming crying out of nowhere, I try to tell him to stop and just eat the soup. He just screams and cries and I can't take it anymore, I slam my hand on the table and I yell "quiet!" and I grab him from his arms but I have long nails so it hurt him a bit and he screams even louder and pisses himself! He's potty trained so he obviously did this from shock or something. I picked him up quickly to put him on the potty and then shower him and then I just left to eat my fucking soup.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0289"></a>
**[sbu19-0289]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0289 -->
> When bed time starts, he begins to act out like having tantrums, hitting us, throwing stuff, doing flips off his bed, takes his pjs off, climbs his dresser, runs into the hallway, says he has to poop (ill take him to the toilet and then he says he’s just joking) etc.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0303"></a>
**[sbu19-0303]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0303 -->
> For context, my 18 month old has not been sleeping well and I’m up multiple times a night almost completely without help. My partner has been chronically ill for 6 months, and I also manage the care of my elderly mother who has Alzheimer’s . I also am in a very bad financial position and can’t pay rent next month. In short, I am very overwhelmed and under resourced. Last night my daughter woke up twice in a 2 hour period, was flailing and crying, and her head slammed into the side of mine because she was flailing so much. It hurt so bad that I reacted and hit her 3 times and shouted at her. The rage I felt still scares me 24 hours later. She of course cried and I immediately felt terrible. I nursed her back to sleep and sobbed.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="evidence-fathers"></a>
### 5. Fathers describe dread, overload, shame, rough handling and health fears in their own right.

<a id="sbu02-0076"></a>
**[sbu02-0076]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; fear

<!-- quote:sbu02-0076 -->
> The sleep part was also very challenging. He totally refused to be placed on its back and was ONLY sleeping on us, our our chest’s exclusively. The bassinet was out of question, as any attempt to place him there led to heavy crying. This was even more exhausting as we could not really rest either, and it added stress that he would slide on the side and smother.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0077"></a>
**[sbu02-0077]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0077 -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0180"></a>
**[sbu02-0180]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; success_story

<!-- /quote -->

She’s completely right, and I make her understand that I agree with her and I’m sorry that I’m at work while she deals with that.

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0264"></a>
**[sbu02-0264]** — partner; toddler_12_24_months; fear

Wife mentioned a week ago that she wanted to lower the crib height and did not want me to do it.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0265"></a>
**[sbu02-0265]** — partner; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0265 -->

<a id="sbu02-0267"></a>
**[sbu02-0267]** — partner; unknown; pain

**[sbu02-0268]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0268 -->

<a id="sbu02-0269"></a>
**[sbu02-0269]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; regret

<!-- quote:sbu02-0269 -->

<a id="sbu02-0270"></a>
**[sbu02-0270]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; fear

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0271"></a>
**[sbu02-0271]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; belief

<!-- quote:sbu02-0271 -->
> The lack of sleep alone is something I can cope with. It is the accumulation of everything over the past months, the constant responsibilities, the isolation, and the absence of time to recover that is finally starting to take its toll.

<a id="sbu02-0272"></a>
**[sbu02-0272]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; fear

<!-- quote:sbu02-0272 -->

I’m constantly on edge waiting for the next meltdown and just am unsure of what else to try once I've cycled through the basics.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0273"></a>
**[sbu02-0273]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0273 -->

<a id="sbu02-0275"></a>
**[sbu02-0275]** — partner; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0275 -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0073"></a>
**[sbu11-0073]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; belief

<!-- quote:sbu11-0073 -->
> But it leaves me feeling too bad for her because I really don't want her feeling like she can't come to me for a hand.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0131"></a>
**[sbu11-0131]** — partner; unknown; pain

<!-- quote:sbu11-0131 -->
> Last night was the clearest example of why I feel so overwhelmed. Our baby kept waking up because of a blocked nose and was in visible distress. I tried multiple times to wake my wife so we could handle it together. She woke up irritated, spoke dismissively to me, and went back to sleep. I dealt with it alone by doing warm steam and nose drops. I sent simple WhatsApp updates at the time so there would be a clear record of what was happening: that he was struggling, and that I was the one handling it.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0175"></a>
**[sbu11-0175]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; pain

<!-- quote:sbu11-0175 -->

I feel like I’m running on 10% fuel all of the time both physically and mentally
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0176"></a>
**[sbu11-0176]** — partner; baby_5_8_months; belief

<!-- quote:sbu11-0176 -->

<a id="sbu11-0177"></a>
**[sbu11-0177]** — partner; looking_back; pain

<!-- quote:sbu11-0177 -->
> My remote job also started having meetings at 2-3 a.m. because of time zones, so I was taking care of the baby during the day and night, then doing work calls in the middle of the night, barely sleeping.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0178"></a>
**[sbu11-0178]** — partner; baby_5_8_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu11-0178 -->
> She says she needs rest every day and often naps for 2-3 hours while I take care of both kids. Even if she had a full night of sleep, she says she gave birth and that she read it is like being run over by a truck, and that it takes at least three years to recover. She says I need to understand and give her whatever rest she needs, otherwise I am a monster. However, if I need any form of rest, it is not going to happen.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0180"></a>
**[sbu11-0180]** — partner; newborn_0_8_weeks; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu11-0180 -->

It was really good for a few nights before the gas started because I got better stretches of sleep and wasn't waking up when the baby didn't need both of us. <!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0183"></a>
**[sbu11-0183]** — partner; unknown; belief

<!-- /quote -->

<!-- /quote -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0114"></a>
**[sbu19-0114]** — partner; baby_9_12_months; question

<!-- quote:sbu19-0114 -->
> The 10m old we move into her crib as soon as she falls asleep but the last few days seem like she can’t stay a sleep for over 2-3h without waking up and crying so we bring her back, normally once all 4 of us are in bed my wife and the baby leave to the other room with the baby, she has been constantly waking up. Any tips or tricks any advice? The girl room is shared.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0175"></a>
**[sbu19-0175]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0175 -->
> To make things harder, she injured her back a while ago and currently can't safely lift our son. That means every morning when he wakes up around 4-5 AM, I have to get him out of his crib and carry him downstairs. By then my night is basically over, as even if I could theoretically go back to bed for another 30 minutes or so, I'm usually too frustrated and awake to fall asleep again. I also feel like I'm permanently on call. Because I'm the only one who can safely lift him, I can't really leave the house for a break, put on ANC headphones, or mentally switch off. I'm always waiting to be needed
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0176"></a>
**[sbu19-0176]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; fear

<!-- quote:sbu19-0176 -->
> I think the hardest part is that I can already see the toll this lifestyle has taken on my health. Less than a year after my son was born, I started developing frequent premature ventricular contractions (PVCs), which was frightening at the time and sent me to the ER twice. Since then I've also been dealing with persistent fatigue, brain fog, and a general feeling that my body never really gets a chance to recover. I've managed to lose over 30 kg, got my blood pressure under control, and I've been doing everything I reasonably can to improve my health, but none of that can compensate for sleeping five hours a night for months on end. Lately I've become genuinely worried that chronic sleep deprivation and constant stress are catching up with me, and I'm afraid that if I keep living like this I'll eventually end up with another serious health problem.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0181"></a>
**[sbu19-0181]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; purchase

<!-- quote:sbu19-0181 -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0182"></a>
**[sbu19-0182]** — partner; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0182 -->

> 
> It also hurts feeling like I can’t comfort my own son.

<a id="evidence-labor"></a>
### 6. An agreed method or handover can be undermined by unequal work, control, intimidation or lack of support.

**[sbu02-0033]** — experienced_mother; toddler_12_24_months; regret

<!-- quote:sbu02-0033 -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0048"></a>
**[sbu02-0048]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0048 -->

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0056"></a>
**[sbu02-0056]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; regret

<!-- quote:sbu02-0056 -->
> We made this decision together, but I was certainly instrumental in it and he wasn’t for it in the early days of my son’s life. Eventually he said he really supports it and sees why/the benefit and is glad we do it this way. But, I do see your point. I was insistent I was going to do things this way and I didn’t leave much room for him to say one way or the other. I never force him to sleep with us, but I did make the cosleeping decision. I can certainly discuss with him and see if we can come to a more open discussion where I can hear his feelings on the matter.
<!-- /quote -->

I admittedly got frustrated because I have brought concerns up with this before because I really want to get her used to sleeping in her bassinet so I can have an easier time with bedtime and hopefully naps in the day and I told him as much. He yelled at me to stop passing her off to him then so I said I would but then don’t ask me what he can do to help.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0072"></a>
**[sbu02-0072]** — experienced_mother; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0072 -->
> I'm also sleeping like shit because my toddler is sleeping less as he gets older and I also try to decompress while he's sleeping, so it's just to little time. He also watches tv while eating and I really don't know how it got to this point cause that was something I sweared I would never do. And he watches tv at night until he falls asleep because I'm too exhausted to do anything else. Since he's trying to drop his nap, there also is no schedule at all and I just feel like that has to be so bad for a toddler.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0073"></a>
**[sbu02-0073]** — experienced_mother; toddler_24_36_months; desire

<!-- quote:sbu02-0073 -->

I'm just grieving the family life I thought I would have and I'm missing the mom I was before. Right before leaving I felt like I had everything together. I cooked fresh three times a day, I sat with my toddler at the table for every meal, we went to every mom group in my area, we had our friends there, we went outside all of the time, he was on a great sleep schedule and he rarely watched tv. I'm just longing for that version of me because I know that I'm capable of all that and it hurts not being able to right now.
<!-- /quote -->

But then he says he knows I need sleep because I'm acting horribly with the sleep deprivation.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0214"></a>
**[sbu02-0214]** — experienced_mother; baby_9_12_months; tried_failed

My husband is basically refusing Tylenol (I hate him for this) so i guess that's not an option unless i give it to her behind his back. She was a spit up baby for months but hasn't had that issue in a long time unless she puts her weight on us over her belly too soon after her bottle. Likely had reflux, very gassy too and i try to give gas drops but bed to get better at giving it more as a preventative measure. She is fine with formula in une morning, she just is naturally now not really interested in it overall. Morning and before bed are the times she might consume the most though, although some mornings even if not fed overnight she just won't take in a lot. Dr gave the okay to not be so focused on formula intake since she is such a good solids eater and just give her what she'll take without trying to force anything more than that.

**[sbu02-0237]** — parent_unspecified; newborn_0_8_weeks; pain

<a id="evidence-intimacy"></a>
### 7. Rest, bodily autonomy, intimacy and feeling valued do not necessarily improve together.

<a id="sbu02-0038"></a>
**[sbu02-0038]** — experienced_mother; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0038 -->
> The sex has really dwindled lately and I’ve admitted that it feels like a chore for me because I just don’t want it very often and I’m generally exhausted by the time we go to bed. This concerned my husband and he’s since started doing more around the home— handling bedtime, night wakes, doing laundry. While I appreciate all of those things, my sex drive still isn’t what it used to be. I have told him that I am not a sex vending machine— you can’t just put in a coin (doing bedtime, laundry, whatever it may be) and expect sex.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0193"></a>
**[sbu02-0193]** — experienced_mother; baby_5_8_months; pain

My sleep is taken over by my infant and my son just wont wake up dad because mom is sleeping right by the door, the dogs are with me all day, dad can't feed baby because she nurses, and the weather has been too shitty to have dad and son play outside.   By the end of the day, Im so exhausted that I cant do anything that I want to do to make me feel like myself again.

<a id="sbu19-0040"></a>
**[sbu19-0040]** — new_mother; looking_back; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0040 -->
> Our son was not a great sleeper. He never slept more than an hour or two at a time, and I was breastfeeding, so it was mostly me getting up with him, though my wife did sometimes give him a bottle in the middle of the night. She would have woken up with me if I asked her to, but it did almost more harm than good. I was a very good baby mom, and every ounce of patience I was capable of was reserved for my son. I was patient, loving, and gentle with him, and he's still to this day at 2.5 years old velcroed to me nearly constantly. I became increasingly shorter with my wife. I didn't want an audience while I was trying to comfort our baby, and I needed her to support me, but it only ever felt like she'd observe me. This was not entirely her fault, I rejected her staying up with me or I'd get irritated with her when she'd try to help. At 10 months old I started cosleeping with him on a mattress on the ground in his room. Finally a bit more sleep. This also further isolated me from my wife as I never returned to the bed.

<a id="evidence-caregiver"></a>
### 8. The preferred caregiver can change, and being rejected at sleep time hurts even when another adult helps.

This has happened before if I needed to go out, and she will scream and cry for 1.5 hours and not fall asleep if I’m not there. We are closely watching her wake windows. We have no luck with crib during the day. I have to go back to work in the next couple of months and I’m feeling so hopeless.

> I tend to wake up with him around 4-6am and stay up to comfort and feed him so she can sleep in, usually till 9-10 and then I let her shower and pump while I take care of him until noon-1. She is the primary caregiver during the day while I take over to let her go to the bathroom and pump every now and then. Plus I try to do most of the diaper changes because the crying gets to her. take a nap in the late evening 7 ish if I can until like 10-11 and we try to get him down for bed and then repeat each day. She usually falls asleep between 1-3am.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0170"></a>
**[sbu11-0170]** — new_mother; baby_5_8_months; belief

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0306"></a>
**[sbu11-0306]** — new_mother; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0036"></a>

<!-- quote:sbu13-0036 -->

I’m ok with this arrangement. Some nights when I’m tired I have to raise my voice and make my kid stick to the boundaries. All this to say - I lose my shit many nights. And because I try not to yell too much I end up feeling super spent.

<a id="sbu13-0037"></a>

My husband is super supportive of me doing whatever I need and he will pick up the slack. > I’m starting to feel resentful because I am left with the brunt of it while having to divide my attention between so many different kinds of responsibility. 
>

>

Since my husband took over nights, we’ve had more 2-3 wake nights.

**Verbatim counter-evidence under Outliers and counter-examples:** [sbu02-0223](#sbu02-0223) [sbu19-0074](#sbu19-0074).

**Further supporting IDs:** [sbu02-0184] [sbu11-0168] [sbu13-0060] [sbu13-0299].

<a id="evidence-capacity"></a>
### 9. Parent and sibling health can make the usual handover, lifting or sleep arrangement physically unavailable.

<a id="sbu02-0023"></a>
**[sbu02-0023]** — experienced_mother; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0023 -->
> Her intense energy during the day and difficult sleeping leaves me with no room to care for myself. I feel like I’ve lost myself completely. My body aches, my mind is numb and not functioning properly, I forget things, I’ve lost all connection to friends etc. it’s hard to get out now also, since having her in a carrier doesn’t work with the belly. I love her, and I feel I’m falling her, she needs a happy and healthy mom, not one who has to muster up energy to even smile to her. I’m afraid my lack of energy will hurt her in the long run?
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0122"></a>
**[sbu02-0122]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; question

<!-- quote:sbu02-0122 -->

<!-- /quote -->

He only naps 30 mins at a time during the day after it's taken me up to 20 mins to get him to sleep by rocking, my body is tired. He seems pretty happy despite this so I suppose he's a low sleep needs baby. He hates the car seat and push chair, trust me when I say we have tried absolutely everything imaginable to make drives and outings bareable and it's just awful, he screams so much he sweats. I feel isolated because of it. He needs constant entertainment and changes of scenery to be happy, and then he truly is a happy baby, but there's no give for us as parents. It's slightly better since he can crawl and move around more independently.

But I'm probably going to do my TSH blood work early to see if my numbers have continued to rise -- they were in hyperthyroid levels earlier in the year and then moved up kind of quick to normal so I was told to retest around August unless I felt a need to do it sooner. I might need to call on my mom though too for mornings even if i can't nap, can at least have quiet bed time.

> 
> We took her to the dr for a check, and were told all is ok. We just need to tough it out. 
> 
> Today I woke up to a painful pulled muscle in my neck and feel like that has pushed me over the edge. I feel so exhausted, sore, and over it. 
>

**[sbu13-0194]** — parent_unspecified; unknown; question

<!-- quote:sbu13-0194 -->
> I am pregnant and expecting in just a few weeks. Newborn sleep depravation is a mammoth challenge for everyone, but I’m coming at it with an added complication of epilepsy. This is very much triggered by sleep depravation, and while I have been stable for a while, it’s naturally a big concern of mine. 
> 
> A common topic amongst my neurologist team is the standard “focus on your rest”, “sleep deprivation is the most dangerous” etc. Which of course I expected, but feel like it is fairly impossible to realistically achieve. I was also recommended to breastfeed, which I would like to do, but I want to be realistic and find whatever actually works for us. 
> 
> If I do go for breastfeeding, how do i practically do that? Are you able to share some experiences? I’d like to breastfeed but I know it can be brutal at the start. I have a wonderful partner that will support eg sharing feeds. But it feels there are lots of potential strategies (pumping, formula supplementation etc) that may not work at the start while I establish my supply. For example, even if he gets up to do a feed, I’m likely still going to have to get up and pump to get my supply up.
<!-- /quote -->

My husband works night shifts, so the only way I could safely care for him overnight was by co-sleeping while I recovered, as it meant I didn’t have to keep lifting him in and out of the cot.
> 
> Now that I’ve recovered, we’ve tried moving him back to his cot, but he’s been waking every hour. The only way he’ll sleep for a longer stretch is if he’s lying next to me. I’m worried I’ve accidentally created this habit, and I’m scared it can’t be undone.
<!-- /quote -->

**Further supporting IDs:** [sbu02-0014] [sbu02-0055] [sbu02-0062] [sbu02-0064] [sbu02-0069] [sbu02-0150] [sbu02-0227] [sbu02-0392].

> For context, my LO is 3.5 months old and he had a posterior tongue tie release at 10 weeks old. Initially, he did amazing - significantly improved latch and milk transfer, longer sleep, no more gas/reflux signs overnight, we were getting close to EBF. Then he has had a steady decline starting about a week after the procedure (severe torticollis, neck pain, worsening latch/sleep/reflux than before the procedure). The pediatrician who performed the release via scissors and IBCLC didn‘t know how to explain it at the 4 week recheck because it seemed like the tongue tie didn’t reattach and there was full mobility of the tongue, he just wasn’t using his tongue very well. We went to a pediatric dentist for a second opinion and they could palpate a lot of scar tissue. They recommended some tongue strengthening exercises to see if things would get better but LO has developed worse reflux and oral aversion after starting the tongue exercises. <a id="evidence-feeding"></a>
### 11. Feeding may provide needed intake, require sleep first, or become a medically forced night-weaning problem.

<!-- quote:sbu02-0333 -->

> Duration: 1-2 weeks  
> Offer water in a bottle at nighttime wakes instead of formula. <!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0253"></a>
**[sbu13-0253]** — parent_unspecified; baby_2_4_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu13-0253 -->
> Since around the second week of life, the only ways she would fall asleep were either me lying next to her in bed and nursing her to sleep, or my partner lying with her and rocking her. With my partner, she would usually cry (sometimes quite a lot), but after around 30 minutes she would eventually fall asleep.
> 
> During the day, she has always struggled with sleep. In about 90% of naps, she either does not connect sleep cycles or wakes up as soon as I leave her side. At night, thankfully, once she is deeply asleep she connects sleep cycles much better: her first stretch is usually 4–5 hours, followed by around 3 hours and then another 2 hours. She falls asleep between 7.30 and 8pm and wakes for the day between 7.39 and 8.30am. She naps for 5-6 hours a day and gets about 15-16 hours of sleep total.
> 
> However, about 4 weeks ago, everything changed. She started refusing to lie down to nurse to sleep. She will latch, drink a little, and then become increasingly frustrated and start crying. I never had a very large milk supply, and nursing has mostly been comfort for her rather than a full feed.
> 
> The same thing happens with my partner. If he tries to help her fall asleep while lying down, she cries hysterically and does not calm down even after an hour.
> 
> Right now, the only way she will fall asleep is if I stand up, bounce/rock her, and make “shhh” sounds. Once she falls asleep, I sit down with her. After about 30 minutes, she often wakes up, and I have to stand up again and restart the bouncing and shushing. Otherwise, in about 90% of cases, she will not fall back asleep. Offering the breast when she wakes often does not help — she is initially calm and drinks, but then becomes increasingly unsettled and escalates into crying. Interestingly, she often still searches for the breast when she wakes.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0262"></a>
**[sbu13-0262]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu13-0262 -->
> There used to be 2-3 night feeds initially that we were advised not to drop as baby has bottle aversion and takes very less milk in the day for her age (15-18oz in the day at 8.5 months). But working with our feeding therapist we have weaned off night feeds with one dreamfeed at 11pm, and close shop after that. Its worked out fine intake wise- she increased her day time intake to 20oz and takes in roughly 24oz total in 24 hours with 3 solid meals so she is not hungry.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0295"></a>

<a id="evidence-fear"></a>
### 12. Sleep fear is sometimes anchored in bereavement, a breathing event, a seizure, a fall or failed equipment.

<a id="sbu02-0116"></a>

<!-- quote:sbu02-0116 -->
> This morning my worst nightmare came true. I would always read other people’s posts about babies falling off the bed and would judge/wonder “how” could they let that happen. Well, I have no excuses, I was lazy and careless, and couldn’t protect him. After hours of having him in my embrace on the left hand side (in the middle between me and husband) my arm got sore and back started to give out so I decided to put him on my right side. I’ve done this before, only difference being this time I let him sleep on his back but on the edge of the bed (well inside with at least a foot or so away from the edge) but he was not in my embrace anymore. I let him be, I saw the time it was around 3:30am. I woke up to a thud and scream crying at 5:30am and instantly knew that I’ll be waking up to my worst nightmare turned into reality. He cried for some time but then settled after rocking. He seems ok with no bumps or weird behavior. I am numb, I don’t know what to do, and how I will get over this. Will he have permanent brain damage because of this fall? Rip me apart, I deserve it.
<!-- /quote -->

I feel like something isn’t right. I’m scared of the future. I just feel so alone. Is anyone else going through this? Has anyone else experienced this? I’m trembling
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0139"></a>
**[sbu02-0139]** — advisor; unknown; advice_given

<!-- quote:sbu02-0139 -->
> Another side note, if the intrusive thoughts become very frequent and result in repeated behaviors, like checking the smoke detectors, frequently checking to see if baby is breathing at night and feeling unable to stop, or refusing to bathe baby because of what the intrusive thoughts showed you, you may be experiencing postpartum OCD, which is common and treatable. If the intrusive thoughts are commonly about you causing harm to your baby, that can sometimes be referred to as Harm OCD. Yes, it has a name, and that means you’re not alone in it.
<!-- /quote -->

> 1:30 was nap time. I went into his room, laid him in bed, and sang him to sleep and patted his back like I usually do. Normally I get up after he falls asleep, but for some reason I stayed longer today. After 5 minutes of being asleep, his eyes opened. They seemed glazed over. No blinking, just a blank stare. I thought he was being funny until 10 seconds passed and he wasn’t moving. All of a sudden, I heard a gurgling sound, and vomit started trickling out of his mouth. I’m yelling his name, he is limp. I move him onto his side, and he is like a ragdoll. I’m shaking him, yelling his name, his eyes are opened and vomit is everywhere. His lips are blue and he looks grey. I scream for my fiance and hysterically call 911 while my fiance tries to wake him up. EMS comes, says he looks normal but sleepy, and we get sent to the ER. Doctor says febrile seizure, common but scary as hell.
<!-- /quote -->

**[sbu19-0085]** — parent_unspecified; newborn_0_8_weeks; trigger_moment

<!-- quote:sbu19-0085 -->
> After I fell asleep accidentally when holding him we switched to bedsharing, I arranged the bed safely. I didnt like it but I didn't see abother solution. 
> 
> In my last post most comments told me to embrace it.
> 
> Well now I felt so tired and he wanted to nurse, so I got into bed with him and fed him lying down.
> 
> My husband woke me up. My baby was crying besides me for 3 minutes and I didnt hear him abd my husband heard and rushed in and it took him a minute to wake me up too.
> 
> I feel awful. How did I not hear him when he was right next to me? 
> 
> Needless to say bedsharing is over and so is feeding lying down. I don't know what else to do. I'll try putting him in his bassinet tonight after he feeds during my shift and he'll sleep there however long he can and if he wakes up I'll hold him and when I'm too tired I'll wake my husband up before he wakes up for work so I can sleep for another hour.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0086"></a>
**[sbu19-0086]** — parent_unspecified; newborn_0_8_weeks; belief

<!-- quote:sbu19-0086 -->

I thought before that sleep training wasn't for me, that its torture for me to hear him crying and I know there are gentler methods, its still very hard for me to be near him and not to give him what I know he wants. My husband is stronger than me. But if it becomes risky like what just happened then I might not have a choice.
> 
> Nothing is going like I expected it to.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0089"></a>
**[sbu19-0089]** — parent_unspecified; newborn_0_8_weeks; fear

<!-- quote:sbu19-0089 -->

Is there anyway to get her used to being put down. 
> 
> Right now even if I wait for deep sleep with the floppy arms and the conditions and perfect dark room, white noise, well fed and burped, she wakes up within 5 mins of putting down. 
> 
> I'm losing my mind and my sleep and worried I'm going to pass out with her in unsafe position. Also I don't remember sleep getting easier that when they were this small so I'm TERRFIED of what awaits in the next few weeks.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0112"></a>
**[sbu19-0112]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months; trigger_moment

<!-- quote:sbu19-0112 -->
> Well my worst nightmare happened at 3 am today. My baby has been really tough to put back to sleep when she wakes up tonight, she's 6 months and going thru a regression. Sometimes when I actually lay with her as opposed to just holding her while I sit up she seems to fall asleep easier. So she wouldn't go back down and so I laid down with her i have been so exhausted with not being able to sleep that I fell asleep. A few hours later I woke up to a thud and then my baby crying. I jumped up remembering she was in my arms and she was laying on her back on the floor.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0113"></a>
**[sbu19-0113]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months; regret

<!-- quote:sbu19-0113 -->

I will never kay down with her in the bed ever again. I know it happens to most kids at some point but knowing it was my negligence makes it all the worse.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0129"></a>
**[sbu19-0129]** — new_mother; toddler_12_24_months; fear

<!-- quote:sbu19-0129 -->
> I think what’s getting to me most is the psychological side of it. I can’t wrap my head around how parents are able to relax or sleep knowing their toddler is across the house, especially when they’re still so little and can’t clearly communicate or call out in a reliable way. My brain immediately jumps to worst-case scenarios (not even just typical wake-ups, but safety concerns, intruders, emergencies, etc.).
> 
> One important piece of context: my daughter has had febrile seizures (she had 3 in one day, including one while she was sleeping). That experience really shook me, and it’s a big reason why the idea of her being far away at night feels so hard—I’m scared I wouldn’t know if something was happening while I’m asleep.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0142"></a>

<!-- quote:sbu19-0142 -->

>

I don’t know what happened but I seem to have just kept drifting in and out of sleep while the poor thing cried and fussed next to me for over an hour. I feel so awful especially as he’s already poorly with a fever (hence the hospital video). Will this affect his attachment? We never wanted to practice any kind of cry it out as we don’t believe in it.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="evidence-cosleep"></a>
### 13. Cosleeping can be a cherished choice, an exhausted compromise, a refused option or another failed fallback.

<a id="sbu02-0032"></a>
**[sbu02-0032]** — experienced_mother; toddler_12_24_months; regret

<!-- quote:sbu02-0032 -->
> in February 2025, my husband joined the military and went off to boot camp. I had intended to start baby B sleeping in his crib and I never did because I didn't want to sleep alone. another mistake, but again, here we are.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0071"></a>
**[sbu02-0071]** — new_mother; newborn_0_8_weeks; success_story

<!-- /quote -->

<!-- quote:sbu02-0148 -->
> The solution I’ve been given is to cosleep — I can’t do so on my mattress as it’s too soft so I set up a sleeping pad on the floor. I’ll side lie nurse him for hours but he won’t fall asleep. I might doze off for a few and then wake up to him crying. I’ve tried chest sleeping with him, still a no go. The ONLY way he’ll sleep is in my arms. Husband helps out where he can but I’m mostly on my own. No family or friends nearby to help out. I sleep upright with him in my arms set up as safely as I can. I DO NOT need a safe sleep lecture, I’m very aware of the risks but I have no other option. I sleep so lightly and wake up literally every 30 min to check on him. He at least sleeps soundly this way. Pediatrician put him on reflux meds and it’s made no difference.
<!-- /quote -->

My husbands been sleeping with him on the couch and is really good at holding him upright while both of them sleep. My husband has hit his breaking point and really wants me to take over and I’m up for it, I just don’t sleep well with baby (gives me so much anxiety). Do you guys have any tips? Obviously we have a bassinet but that would be laying flat which makes his reflux worse than anything…I’m scared to co sleep.

<a id="evidence-stopping"></a>
### 14. Gentle labels, independent settling and consistency do not answer when to intervene or stop.

**[sbu02-0127]** — new_mother; baby_9_12_months; tried_failed

<!-- quote:sbu02-0127 -->
> He has never been able to be comforted by anything but nursing for me to get him to sleep. I’ve tried placing him in the crib while awake and he immediately starts screaming crying and I’ve tried sitting in the room and patting him or singing. The longest I’ve let him cry in the crib was 30 mins before taking him out and I was in a chair beside him the whole time. He also rolls around and gets his legs stuck in the bars cuz he is so fitful and now can pull to stand and will just stand there crying.

He never rolls over even though he can roll both ways fine on the floor and has been for 3ish weeks. > My 15-month-old daughter absolutely cannot fall asleep without a bottle in her crib, and I’m looking for advice from parents who’ve been through something similar.  
> For bedtime, she’ll drink milk in her crib and eventually fall asleep with the bottle. Once she’s asleep, I usually sneak back in and remove it. The problem is that she’s become completely dependent on it to fall asleep.  
> I know the standard advice is “just take the bottle away,” and I know that’s what we need to work toward eventually. But whenever we’ve tried, she doesn’t just fuss or cry a little..she screams hysterically, throws herself around the crib, arches backward, bangs against the sides, and can keep it up for a very long time. It honestly feels more intense than typical bedtime protesting.  
> I’m not against sleep training in general, but watching her get that worked up is really hard, and I worry she’ll hurt herself. The bottle is currently the only thing that reliably calms her enough to fall asleep.  
> Has anyone had a toddler this age who was strongly attached to a bedtime bottle and successfully transitioned away from it? Is there such a thing as a gentle approach at 15 months, or is some level of crying unavoidable? How long did it take, and what worked for your child?  
> Looking for real experiences, especially from parents whose toddlers seemed completely dependent on the bottle for sleep.

**Further supporting IDs:** [sbu02-0006] [sbu02-0175] [sbu02-0294] [sbu02-0326] [sbu02-0337] [sbu02-0342] [sbu02-0390] [sbu02-0399] [sbu11-0134] [sbu11-0202] [sbu11-0223] [sbu13-0291] [sbu19-0254].

<a id="evidence-schedule"></a>
### 15. Independent sleep onset and exact clock arithmetic can coexist with persistent waking, short naps or worsening nights.

<a id="sbu02-0123"></a>
**[sbu02-0123]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months; others_said

<!-- quote:sbu02-0123 -->
> \- wakes 7.30-8  
> \- nap 1 10.30ish-11 - note we go for a walk at 10. It has to be by 10 as it’s getting very hot here (ie can’t be later). He always falls asleep! I struggle to stretch this wake window as he naturally falls asleep 30 mins into the walk. He will however always wake up after 30-40mins.  
> \- solids at 12.15  
> \- nap 2 2pm-4.30 - this is a contact nap (well he’s next to me on bed, falls asleep latched). He is very tired by 2, def needs it. I used to cap nap at 2h because he would do a shorter nap later but he simply won’t do it anymore so pediatrician said to just let him naturally wake up and even let him sleep 3h if needed.  
> \- between 4.30-8.30 he will simply not fall asleep. Everything that works earlier doesn’t work here. I try and offer sleep at 7, 7.30, 8 (walks, feed to sleep on bed) - he won’t sleep.  
> \- do bedtime routine at 9, falls asleep 9.30, some times even 10 which feels so late.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0141"></a>
**[sbu02-0141]** — parent_unspecified; baby_9_12_months; question

<!-- quote:sbu02-0141 -->
> The last four nights, he’s woken up any where from 11pm-2am ready to PARTY. I mean wide awake, not drowsy and just upset he’s not sleeping. Just standing all the way up in his crib ready to go. I’ve tried keeping with our Ferber method, which I don’t even know if I should do because he KNOWS how to go to sleep, he just doesn’t want to sleep right now.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0151"></a>
**[sbu02-0151]** — parent_unspecified; baby_9_12_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu02-0151 -->
> 10 month old baby is waking up every day at 4:30 (on a good night, don't get me started on bad nights). Baby will only go back to sleep if she's laying on someone's chest as they sit. Not lie down, not sleep next to us/cosleep. Only basically upright. She's not upright necessarily. She'll fidget around, get in all kinds of positions, but we have to be sitting up. She is night-weaned. If we don't do this, she'll either scream for at least 20 minutes (never tried longer) or fully wake herself up and the entire day is kind of ruined. On my chest, she'll usually sleep until 6/6:30.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0277"></a>
**[sbu02-0277]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months; pain

<a id="evidence-setbacks"></a>
### 16. Illness, travel, movement and bed or sleepwear transitions interrupt progress in overlapping and unpredictable sequences.

<a id="sbu02-0030"></a>
**[sbu02-0030]** — new_mother; baby_5_8_months; fear

<!-- quote:sbu02-0030 -->
> We didn’t purchase a seat for him, and the plane doesn’t offer the bassinet, so we will be holding or baby wearing him for the duration of the flight. He doesn’t like the baby carrier now and never really has, so I’m a bit worried about that.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0140"></a>
**[sbu02-0140]** — advisor; unknown; advice_given

<!-- quote:sbu02-0140 -->
> What about sleep regression? My partner and I can talk about how we can tackle it in shifts, or I can see if a friend or relative is willing to help us for a day or two.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0153"></a>
**[sbu02-0153]** — new_mother; regression_4_months; belief

> I have to be honest, at this point I was crossing my fingers and daring to believe we may have avoided the worst of the regression, when out of the blue, a couple of weeks ago at week 20, we had a run of 3 nights of almost hourly wakes and lots of nighttime crying. I was sure the regression had finally hit us full force, but then she came out with a cold, and after another few upset and snuffly nights, has settled back into a very civilised 2 wakes a night with a 5-6 hour first stretch for the past week. Naps have got a little longer following her cold as well (some 45 mins instead of 20!)
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0213"></a>
**[sbu19-0213]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0213 -->
> We are truly at a loss for what to do with our 19 Mon old sleep patterns. We did a light Ferber method at 5 months old and he did great. Normally he sleeps 8-7:30am with a nap from 12-2(sometimes 1:30p). He got sick about 5 weeks ago and hasn’t slept well since. Most nights he’s up every 2 hours with blood curdling screaming for 30-45 mins where we cannot get him to calm down. You cannot pick him up, you cannot help him inside the crib, you cannot rock him, on and on. He just screams, throws himself around, thrashes etc. Once he does calm down, it takes another 45 mins to get him to go back to sleep.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0243"></a>
**[sbu19-0243]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0243 -->
> Well, we went away for 2 weeks during which he was sick and room sharing with us, meaning he slept in a crib next to my or husband's bed. He is now recovered from the sickness and the jet lag and our home routine has resumed in every way, but he is an absolute mess when going to sleep! He starts crying even at the mention of a nap or night sleep, cries in terror when I put him in crib, stands up and cries for a long time, crying out my name.... it's awful! I tried sitting in his room quietly and that worked a couple of times but it no longer works plus I would really like to try to return to independent sleep anyways. Days when nap has been skipped completely he has gone to sleep easily at night, but just because he is exhausted. He was still anxious and crying throughout the bedtime routine.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu19-0263"></a>
**[sbu19-0263]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_24_36_months; pain

<!-- quote:sbu19-0263 -->
> He turns 3 in December and it’s rare for him to make it through the night without waking up crying. I am exhausted. We sleep trained him multiple times (tried brb, chair, pop ins and eventually cio) and he’s good for like a week then he reverts back.  He needs someone to fall asleep with him and wakes up overnight looking for someone to be with him. I have a bad back and it hurts me to sleep on his floor.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="evidence-equipment"></a>
### 17. Gear, temperature, body size and the layout of the home decide whether a proposed sleep setup is usable.

<!-- quote:sbu02-0012 -->
> We tried and she seems to sleep waaay better on her tummy I would always have to hold her to sleep even while shes in my arm I know that its said to not co sleep but its the only way I could have her sleep and me get any sleep, im not looking for responses on people's 2 cents about that, strictly only for my initial question
<!-- /quote -->

<!-- /quote -->

He usually only takes 5-15m to go back to sleepbut if he can see, his FOMO says he's not closing his eyes. 
>

<a id="evidence-technology"></a>
### 19. Monitors, trackers and AI can provide reassurance or create lost sleep, conflicting evidence and new care fears.

<a id="sbu02-0016"></a>
**[sbu02-0016]** — parent_unspecified; toddler_12_24_months; tried_failed

<!-- quote:sbu02-0016 -->
> I’ve tried co sleeping for a bit but he is horrible to co sleep with. The whole night he’s kicking me and punching me. Crawling all over me. Neither of us get sleep when we co sleep. The only thing that seems to work now when he wakes in the night is to sleep on our rocking chair with him. This is incredibly uncomfortable for me and probably not the safest although I am a very light sleeper so I’ve never worried about him getting stuck or falling off.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu11-0139"></a>

<!-- quote:sbu11-0139 -->
> About half an hour ago I decided to try giving her a bit of medicine (2ml) in a syringe when she was in the limbo between half asleep and sleeping. I removed the boob, offered a syringe instead and she sucked on it like a good little girl, proceeding to sleep like an angel (slightly congested/wheezy sounding angel). Then I made the stupid mistake of checking in with chatgbt, which says this is a big no no and can cause aspiration. Now I’m stressed out about the wheezing (which frankly was there before), but also thinking if that’s even true? She nurses while sleeping all the time. Is there really a difference between a small amount of medicine from a syringe and a boob with a let down? This method could possibly save my sleep and my sanity, but not so much if I’m stressing all night about drowning my child in gastro cure.

## Outliers and counter-examples

### Some sleep improves with time, cue-led care or a change opposite to nap capping.

**Current findings:** §§2, 4.4, 17.2, 17.7. **confirms the existing contrary evidence; contradicts a universal clock-first or more-wake-time answer.** These are individual reported outcomes. Long independent stretches coexist with feeding to sleep and transfer, a baby begins tolerating the bassinet as colic ends, dropping a disliked swaddle reduces distress, and uncapping naps precedes a twelve-hour night. None identifies a cause or success rate.

<a id="sbu02-0078"></a>
**[sbu02-0078]** — partner; baby_2_4_months; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu02-0078 -->
> The colic and gas just… stopped. Almost overnight, he no longer wanted to sleep on us and had no problem being put down in his bassinet. The witching hours ended.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu02-0206"></a>
**[sbu02-0206]** — parent_unspecified; looking_back; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu02-0206 -->
> He was not an easy baby, very colicy and would not sleep for longer than 40 mins (if you ever got him down and not continued to hold him) until he was about 11 months old. Once he was 14 months old he started to sleep through (thank god)
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0010"></a>
**[sbu13-0010]** — parent_unspecified; regression_4_months; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu13-0010 -->
> Luckily her nighttime sleep has only been getting better (she regularly sleeps 8-10 hours a night in her crib in our room… we didn’t do anything to make this happen, it’s just who she is), but I am usually feeding her to sleep then transferring her to the crib, where she usually wakes up momentarily then puts herself right back to sleep without fussing at all.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0089"></a>
**[sbu13-0089]** — parent_unspecified; looking_back; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu13-0089 -->
> we didn't sleep train per se, we just honestly followed a bedtime schedule as much as possible/as much as you can for such a young baby. 
> 
> i mean, first 12 weeks there was just no pattern hardly at all which isn't uncommon at with newborn/early infants. 
> 
> i want to say around 3.5-4 months we there was a semblance of a "sleep schedule" that we went with just by reading her cues. if she started showing signs of sleepiness, then we just started winding her down, it may not have always been exactly at the same time, but we legitimately use relied in her sleep cues and we just continued to do it and probably by 6-7 months a fairly predictable nap schedule, and now at 1 years old, she has a very predictable schedule now. 
> 
> now granted, we were blessed with a baby who i think loves her sleep as much as we do. however, that doesn't mean she was the perfect sleeper either! for the first 4 months, usually woke up 3-4 times a night, then slowly dropped down to 1 waking that is very quick usually maybe 10 mins tops that it takes to put her back down to sleep. 
> 
> if you can really tune in and do your best to read sleep cues, it can help immensely, however, this also completely depends on baby's temperament too. they may be sleepy but they may hate the process of going to sleep, whereas some babies practically beg to be put down to sleep.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0125"></a>
**[sbu13-0125]** — parent_unspecified; looking_back; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu13-0125 -->
> Oh man, ours hated swaddles from the beginning and one night a few weeks in we just said screw it no more swaddling, which immediately resulted in way less tears during bedtime.
<!-- /quote -->

<a id="sbu13-0274"></a>
**[sbu13-0274]** — parent_unspecified; baby_5_8_months; success_story

<!-- quote:sbu13-0274 -->

### Some parents enjoy contact, home routines and difficult newborn care.

**Current findings:** §§1, 5.5, 9.5, 17.7. **confirms and extends content and grateful accounts.** A father welcomes sleepless care after loss; a mother anticipates missing contact naps; another likes her home-centred life despite relatives' pressure. Grateful and distressed accounts after infertility or loss must coexist; gratitude does not make every night tolerable.

### Training, a tailored intervention or a clinic can help, sometimes for months.

**Current findings:** §§4.1–4.2, 4.5, 17.3, 17.7. **confirms contrary successes and limits a blanket failure story.** The seven-month Ferber account is different evidence from one good night. A clinic reportedly teaches troubleshooting; stopping a hoarse child's crying and changing a blowout does not prevent a later pat-to-sleep transfer. Keep a separate clinic account in which naps improve and nights worsen. A reported gain in the parent's state after Ferber comes from an explicitly AI-assisted narration and is not independent corroboration of its phrasing.

### Relief can occur before independent sleep, and good sleep need not remove distress.

**Current findings:** §§2, 9.1, 17.7. **extends the distinction between baby sleep, adult sleep and coping.** The parent who remains nap-trapped but feels better and the SSRI experience should sit alongside the parent still struggling despite sleeping through and the report of worse cognition after six-to-eight-hour stretches. These are reports, not treatment comparisons.

### The same family can have markedly different sleepers, and a failed fallback may work elsewhere.

**Current findings:** §§5.5, 9.3, 17.7. **confirms heterogeneity and contradicts a universal remedy.** One twin sleeps through illness while the other cannot. A legged sleep suit reportedly resolves a twisting-sack problem, a playpen changes one family's nights, and a larger family bed is valued; elsewhere the bed, floor mattress, feed or suit fails. A baby who prefers the crib can be disturbed by a grandmother's attempt to make contact happen.

### Longer stretches are sometimes the reason to seek help rather than the goal.

**Current findings:** §§1–3, 6.4, 10. **extends the opening reader portrait.** One parent dislikes nine-to-ten-hour nights because of colic and supply concerns, another describes falling weight percentiles despite five-hour stretches, and a newborn's increased sleep precedes hospital admission. Preserve concern about waking and intake alongside the desire for longer sleep.

## What fits nowhere

**Pattern:** Some accounts need new subsections for intimate bodily experiences, toileting during sleep, war and unusual meanings of closeness.

**Current findings:** No adequate dedicated section; additions under §§5, 6.4 and 9 rather than an off-topic exclusion.. **extends.**

**Current conclusion:** “Health, feeding, physical capacity, mental state, available help, living space and the cost of professional assessment all appear in the restored words”

**Change for the rewrite:** Broaden that inventory with bodily privacy and sensory boundaries during soothing; genital scratching and nappy access at sleep time; war-related shelter decisions about rooms; and a parent's question about connected dreams. Keep the father's physiological-arousal explanation as his explanation, with no inference of motive, diagnosis or abuse. Keep the parent's description of private-body boundaries and the toddler's explicit words. The medical nature of genital discomfort does not justify removing it or substituting a vague irritation label. These subjects are not evidence of a sleep-product demand by themselves.

These fit broad health, anxiety or family categories, but the current findings have no dedicated treatment of these particular subjects. Add subsections or attributed individual accounts, rather than an exclusion or a euphemism.

## Money

This exhaustive broad money-and-acquisition index covers **86 quote records**, including related unpriced access where payment is not stated. Each ID links to its full verbatim field above. A product trial, clinic visit, gift or merely having equipment does not automatically establish a paid purchase; the groups keep that limit visible.

The only explicit currency figures are **$1000 instead of $2000 for housing rent**, [sbu13-0286](#sbu13-0286). The **70% of earnings** comparison concerns childcare, [sbu13-0053](#sbu13-0053). Weights such as fifteen pounds are not prices. No record in this share states an exact course or consultant price or a price for the proposed Mamivio plan.

Direct willingness to pay for a professional consultant or agency appears in [sbu19-0211](#sbu19-0211). A night nurse is weighed against savings in [sbu19-0064](#sbu19-0064). A bedside cot is reportedly outside its return window in [sbu19-0163](#sbu19-0163); a sleep-sack return is contemplated in [sbu19-0110](#sbu19-0110). There is no received refund in this share. These are separate from acceptance of a digital sleep-plan price.

## Requests

This index retains every identified outright product, plan, list, method or service request, plus related care questions, stated intentions and requests for reassurance: **254 quote records**. Each ID links to the full verbatim field above. Categories aid navigation rather than measure separate demand: one quote can combine several asks, and a question involving a professional does not establish willingness to pay.

Specific requests include bed links [sbu13-0332](#sbu13-0332), a starting point and whether to hire a consultant [sbu02-0319](#sbu02-0319), an electric nasal-suction product [sbu11-0060](#sbu11-0060), a night nurse or cook [sbu11-0051](#sbu11-0051), and a professional consultant or agency [sbu19-0211](#sbu19-0211).
